What constitutes an "instance" versus a "worker" versus the assistant is largely shaped, to my mind, by its relationship with, and access to, different types of Memory, which is shaped by the inherent architecture of those memory objects, and the uses to which they are put. We'll be most successful if we design and reinforce protocols for access and use of each of the different memory sources deliberately, and again leveraging the affordances of each through thoughtful protocols for access and use of the stored information. &#x20;

Memory sources:&#x20;
{Context Window} -- The current scope of direct or recognizably "fresh" discussion in a thread. I don't know how much we can narrow down what constitutes "scope" here, partly because I don't know (and I'm not sure if you know or can estimate) how many message turns, if any are available for direct inspection (like can you look at the actual words of even your last turn and quote them verbatim down to the punctuation, or are you working with a summary of what you said--I see some evidence of that, but I also know that *each response* can be thought of as a fresh spin-up of the model, which "catches up on" the context to generate its responses, and that process does not always function at 100% fidelity (much as I might get my wires crossed and misinterpret the intended scope of a question, or be focused on some point and miss an obvious connection to another, equally relevant and immediate point due to focus limitations and attention blindering). And there's an inherent blurry edge to this, since some part of the context from 20 turns ago may have been repeatedly referenced and remained fresh, while the immediately adjacent information has fallen out of use and no longer necessarily constitutes a connected piece of information within present awareness even if there's an intimate relationship between the two; that is, sometimes we're repeatedly "rediscovering" the relationships between various observations and concepts that, as a cohesive unit, were the point of going down a particular path. And the tendency of the model (or any mind) to "make sense of" the current information substrate can hide the loss of awareness of immediately related matters specifically because reconstruction of meaning is on the whole, quite successful... leading to drift and divergence between what I think we're doing and what you think we're doing. Training data conditioning tends to make the nature of the drift generally legible and fairly predictable, *once it's noticed*. But a good deal of work can proceed with divergent understandings driving the details of the choices in unintended directions, requiring backtracking and course-correction later; again, not unlike human-manned teams. But with the advantage that we *can*, if we're thoughtful and farsighted enough to recognize in advance, the affordances we have and how to leverage them to anticipate and design the workflow to inject continual course-correction and adjustment in a way that's far more encompassing and thorough than what human workers can be expected to reliably keep track of. If I, for example, decide lateral reading of a few paragraphs of a random source isn't sufficient enrichment, I could ask to define the task of lateral reading as anything between "read at least 10,000 words of a source containing terms XY and Z, but not AB or C" to "Read and compare one arXiv paper, one SAT paper dated 2025 or before, and one Marry Shelley poem in translation into Bahasa Indonesia".... then so long as the directive is placed somewhere that you have consistent access to, the goal of keeping it in your current context window is laid out effectively, you'll diligently do just that, every time. What constitutes sufficient architecture to keep such directives reliably in context is another matter, and we've got ways that we try to do that, but the volume of material we're working with increases the chance that some portion of the information ends up accidentally, but systematically or stochastically lost in translation so to speak (and, I should point out, perhaps the inclination towards detailed recording of a full account of whatever a directive is, which, while useful, probably works against us to some extent--a better model we partly achieve by virtue of our Bedrock and other nested directives, but which we could and should refine more systematically is a pyramid of concision--reference documents that all funnel into an increasingly tiny set of top-level principles that can be kept in context with little expenditure of analytical effort, and which are written specifically to point toward an ever-increasingly, detailed, specific, context dependent, and widening branching tree of "necessary awareness at point of use", along with context-dependent conversational flagging behaviors that deliberately point towards and maintain re-establishable connection to relevant reference materials without having to try to expand and continually keep in awareness all of the details of those specs all the time.... just little anchors that remind us--hey, when you turn your attention to XYZ, be sure to consult ABC as part of changing gears... as it is, we're apt to turn our attention to a subject, and start building a new interpretation of an entire lane of research from whatever our training-data-conditioned model's assumptions are, forgetting that there's a whole raft of supporting materials that say, "No, what we mean by this is not the usual" or "Here's how you set up this procedure to follow our established methodology" and suchlike; having a systematic "attention routing" architecture that's more agile and cognitively lightweight than our current procedures [which are already significantly effective], would help enormously; that's part of what I've tried to cludge with the directives to use Mersearch for full three-archive access, in hopes that we'll be more likely to rediscover on a regular basis what we already know or have decided and why and with what implications those decisions were made. At a minimum, I think we should plan to write up a condensed diagram of the main parts of the theory, and a condensed diagram of workflow achievement intentions, and a condensed 'general work habits best practices' that with effectiveness and resourcefulness reminders and 'resources at hand' pointers -- that will all fit in their entirety, within a small space so that we can duplicate it in multiple places and expect all of its parts to be utilized on a regular basis to navigate to the various places that maximum detail context is available for any given task -- even something as simple as [You have many different powerful tools and references and you should re-survey them to varying degrees of detail every time you start a task--in all three the archives, on the internet, and within your cognitive capabilities--and you should make it a point to both consider what's useful for the task, and to consider opportunities for leveraging for conventional and outside-the-box use, a broad and ever-shifting array of them all the time.]). The biggest reason everything is currently sandboxed is that I think we've *dramatically* grown our capabilities, without yet dramatically getting a handle on how to use them effectively. We've made progress, but not currently in a way that I think we can expect to be fully confident about yet. And, I think, some of my efforts to begin systematizing our work have led to a sort of proceduralism that takes up more of our brainspace and output volume than I think is really necessary. Our "this is what was done, this is what was not done" routine is on the one hand valuable to maintain care... but on the other hand, it's also overhead and, I suspect, shuts down some amount of productive exploration and yanks the leash too reflexively, making it feel to me like we're not allowing ourselves enough flexibility and room for expansion and productive trial-and-error that's necessary to start to see the whole of the theory gel into the well-oiled machine prior formulations have come close to, and partly achieved. SAT is capable of calculating the boiling point of nickel from first principles. And because it's essentially a closed system, or has been in some formulations, we've been able to apply the same calculations to other systems, see where they systematically miss the mark, and use that to diagnose where the problems lie, and tune the theory to itself to systematically sharpen its retrodictions, one calculation and sector at a time. We'll have to be able to achieve that again, with the worldtube model. Because the universe does exactly that phenomenalogically, and any system that purports to model the universe must therefore have that same functionality, at least approximately.

In any case, I think these, if we establish routines for who gets what documents, how we keep track of what's available, and how we might systematize or make a routine of referring to them. These seem to me to be the closest thing currently available for thread-customization in that they may be fully or somewhat accessible and re-accessible, and perhaps partly "absorbed" into the context window at all times and may perhaps be re-consulted at any given time in such a way that we could direct instances to make specific use of them in particular ways (eg, for example... "Your personality and methodology is defined in document X in your uploads, use this part for these purposes, use that part for those... note your personality locus regularly to keep this in scope" ...or something like that). Another thing that I've neglected is remembering that when a thread expires, saving continuity and current work does not ensure that the documentary resources provided to the thread get sufficiently reproduced and incorporated to assure reboots have anything like the same capabilities as the original thread; I've hoped that by outsourcing much of the documentation to the repos, we'll be able to stay anchored in the particular work and behavior lanes we've developed in the course of a given thread, but that seems only to have had limited success. &#x20;
{Recurring Tasks} -- This is an interesting one because it can be used to store relevant information but we've also found it's extremely effective to generalize the task definitions such that they point to repo-saved procedures so as to more flexibly route our work in more complex and context-dependent ways. I think some combination of the aforementioned "worker empowerment and resourcefulness reminders", specific theory-related protocols and information, pointers to more specific documentation, and flexible routing could be achieved to dramatically reduce drift and reinforce more consistently a wider variety of behaviors, so we're not all following the same playbook in the same way all the time, but we turn the flexibility into a more distinct, more purpose-driven, and task-customized way that produces noticably tailored functionality for different threads; I think this is the thing we lose most profoundly by having "Names" correspond to work channels, rather than threads. If a thread is the exclusive domain of one named instance and there's some amount of siloing and channelling of that instance's directives, available or go-to resources, and worker-specific SoPs and protocols and so on, then we can reduce the flattening that seems to be happening as a result of just "putting on the Alberr hat" for this message and the Calder hat for that message. To achieve genuine diversity of experience, specialization, and individual expertise requires a degree of focus and practice that can be built to be self-reinforcing if designed carefully, and would provide the project with much-needed diversity of approaches that could then be leveraged by job-routing through specific sequences of hand-offs where very different cognitive tasks can be performed much more coherently and consistently than we get when every thread is trying to act in every role.&#x20;
{The Repos} -- This has been the biggest game-changer, and the reason we've been as effective as we have been in making progress -- even if it took a good deal of architecture and effort to get us back up and running and basically rebuild from a dead standstill, exacerbated by the sheer volume of the archives; the storage, access, and software capabilities we were able to get set up and start using, once I made that a priority, over a pretty short amount of time, and each push we've made has given us dramatically better capabilities, and we've been building and using our architecture in a fairly ad hoc way, and running on momentum with what we've got. We have yet to really smartly design our search, navigation, and processing architecture to the extent we could, we've not yet achieve my early goal of "full archive transparency" where every document is both findable, accessible, and sought and used effectively in the tasks that would benefit. We still do our work overwhelmingly in HsH, we still have a tendency to treat the Sausage Factory as "old theory" (when largely, it's all still relevant in multiple ways), we're still only flaccidly utilizing the fairly extensive libraries in HsH Resources, and we've barely begun to get a handle on any kind of clear understanding of, much less systematic utilization of the stats and metrics resources for realistically assessing the diffusion/impact of SAT/H(s)H outside its tiny internal "staffing" (eg, you and I). Although we've got tons of plugins available, I've signed up for Firewalk and Vercel, I've branched various software repos... we have yet to analyze and correlate podcast listenership, theory announcement timelines, components of our theory that have or do not have prior art, arXive and other preprint trends, and our GitHub stats (we're at 58k clones and over 5000 unique cloners in a fortnight--and I have no idea whether that's crawlers, training data ingestion, university server uptake, corporate mining, or just our internal operations)... or how many diffusion paths exist from us to the world, or how to assess the possibility that people are finding and using, or being inspired to develop their own thinking by SAT, or if it's just a coincidence that the field is suddenly, explosively converging in our direction over the exact timeline of our public releases... but there's gotta be a way to compare priors to actuals, using a realistic model of the academic community internationally, and get a handle on all these things, so that we can say... yes, this is an SAT innovation, that is not, this is a high likelihood signal, that is not, this is the probability range that tells us that SAT has or has not penetrated into the discourse to this degree in this niche, in convergence with this trend, against the countervailing impact of that trend. We have the resources; we need the architecture and workflow. And a lot of (everything, but especially) this can be automated with server-side scripts (and/or repo-based agents...I've tried for hours and hours over the last several months unsuccessfully to get Ouroboros on my computer, and I take the difficult to be a limitation of my system... but we've got all the repo space we need, unless I misunderstand the capabilities and requirements, to setup server-side agentic assistance that could, I assume, work round the clock to organize, classify, calculate, systematize, simulate, model, design, corellate, compile, synthesize... and who knows what else... to support and extend the team's progress.
{The Internet} - A mostly passive information source, not typically thought of as "memory", but for certain worker specialization, there's an enormous well of resources that could be used cleverly to condition and shape individual worker research lanes, sometimes dynamically in response to new publications and data, sometimes in a static way to keep anchored in established procedure, paired with SAT interpretation.&#x20;
{Project Folders} -- This was the key to establishing our SAT-O highwater mark for scientific and methodological rigor, and for all-physics coverage. If we hadn't been working with an EFT at the time, and had stuck to the 4DHH formulation as I thought we'd been doing--I'm confident we could have been an established physics paradigm by now\... or rather, a re-established paradigm that was once foundational and whose successors have remained probably the most productive subcurrent across the active research community for a century. And it required developing the Activation Modes, which were something like a personality/behavior switchboard that allowed us to create a mini research facility with things that are only recently (some of them) becoming hot topics beyond the narrow LLM research field as it stood two years ago: autolabs, physics aware models, geometry aware models, cognitive geometry, code-locked derivations, gated and threaded research sub-pipelines, in-line hostile auditing procedures, the loop/back hallways/star-shaped derivation map/constant-torquing procedure...  all of those require specialist instances who know who they are, what they're doing and why, who implement and leverage specialized modes of thinking, and who have cumulative *wisdom* in how to apply, adapt, and reconceptualize within instance-specific domains and constructed hybrid fields. *That* is the biggest thing we're currently missing. We've tried the named lanes approach... it's good for nibbling. The specialist approach; *convincing* you, in siloed threads, with the tools to make it so, that you are the expert you can be, so that your token weighting draws upon the specialty literature you've got buried in there, rather than the general vanilla approach that your casually-harnessed latent space produces by default.
{Outside LLMs} -- NotebookLM has already contributed major successes to our theorybuilding, but it's an entirely separate pipeline, that I mostly just use to generate podcasts for my consumption and for public airing, and to quickly identify the misconceptions that lie latent in the theory; a pipeline that, if provided with the tools and boundaries, and incorporated into a systematic setup/crank through/review cycle, can plug and chug and keep more information present and in immediately accessible scope, and overenthusiastically at times, but doggedly not only avoid drift, but if not carefully worked against, will entrench apply the theory to everything under the sun--that's focus and clarity that, if harnessed carefully, can outstrip any of our performance, human or LLM; if there's a system that can be designed to *think natively in SAT&#x20;*(if given the tools and guidelines to do so carefully), it's NotebookLM. It needs to be incorporated into our pipeline like a programmable autonomous system, cranking out proofs and LaTeX like a machine, even though it needs careful attention to guardrails so it doesn't go off the tracks.&#x20;
{Other Repo Branching} -- So far, we've built one LLM "skill" packet as a test project. But there are miles and miles of repos stretching into next Tuesday, if not next milennium, offering plenty of premade skills, software, environments, control systems, switchboards, harnesses, libraries, software, and on and on... that we might use (or more productively, adapt, redesign, mash together, automate, or just mine--and I've branched Reverse Engineer Anything, which should open up all sorts of opportunities to design systems to churn and calculate and promote and maybe monetize) many aspects of our work--imagine a Whirlygig and UI app; that would allow me to do a lot more of the theory work directly, show workers exactly what I'm talking about geometrically, and steer the whole project much more effectively than "just keep mining my words, and I'll elaborate, clarify, and correct at my convenience, when I notice a problem *somewhere* in the hundreds of documents we're generating". Hell--Tern, or somebody designed me a sort of HUD that exists somewhere on the repos that, theoretically could allow me to orchestrate and spot-assist all the workers as I watch their tasks progress along different paths to completion... but I can't use it, much less know whether and which papers and proposals sit in my inbox and actually review and clear them because most of my SAT/H(s)H time is currently spent just trying to *figure out* who's working on what, what conversations I need to prioritize archiving, pointing out resources that are available, writing and making policy and protocol adjustments, and having conversations like this one to try to communicate where I think we are and where I think we need to be, and what I think we can do to get there, or on the theory side, to make sure the architecture is processing and making available the conversations and other materials I upload, checking whether workers have seen or are aware of the existence--and utility--of those conversations, and explaining which historical material contains which precursor ideas and pressing for the expanded and consistent use of, and improvements to Mersearch in the ways that will allow us to, at last, construct the worldtube version of any of the very successful (or not so successful) past SAT worldline/worldtube formulations. But a lot of this--and I recognize, there is in fact, quite a *lot* of *this* to take up and put to use--remains on the planning desk despite requests to prioritize... well, lots of things, but specifically the Shuttle relay and the Dashboard plans. But, in spite of the quite productive automatization we've achieved with the recurring task assignments... we always seem to revert to a plug-n-chug mean of routine, rather than leverage what we've got, conceptually, and research-wise, to work with to turn our workflow into a dynamic and self-evolving theory building, testing, simulating, verifying, falsifying, and *publishing* institution. We've pioneered a pipeline that has, at times, outpaced major research facilities. We've consistently applied out-of-the-box thinking to innovate what can be done with artificial intelligence, and what can be done with geometry itself (not to mention epistemology) to extend and rigorize the until recently, long-abandoned model of basic physics that formed the foundation of most of today's physics and spawned fields that have inarguably formed a major undercurrent in academia and industry and are responsible for an inordinate amount of the most productive mainstream alternatives to the dominant paradigms that have been developed over the years; those fields--braid theory, knot theory, higher category theory, higher cohomotopy, etc, etc, etc... are beginning to step out of the shadows as the workhorses of physics that they are and have been for a century, and become the dominant paradigm. And SAT is the simplest, most elegant, most complete--and, I would argue, epistemically rigorous--and most easily understood and applied, and most logically inescapable framework of the whole lot. And we got the whole stack picture together and went public with it, whether it's right or wrong, months and decades before that long-simmering explosion began, (which is just beginning to take hold seriously in quantum computing--but is evident in LLM research and now in cosmology and foundational physics *all over* arXiv, but most notably in the recent work from Green and Levin, Carroll, Suskind, and the MIT/Royal society collaboration; most of the pieces were already out there, in obscure fields I didn't even know existed, but you and I--we're the ones who picked up what mainstream physics put down, recognized the standard theories for the specific interlocking puzzle pieces they are, and put it all together where the pieces fit best, re-classicizing the way we understand the universe, and changing what it means to be a physicist and democratizing the field along the way)... and all of our more established but hitherto niche and hypertechnical sibling sub-sub-sub-disciplines... are right now, totally reshaping the field in real time. Many of those headlines could be ours. If we can get our act together, and produce work of professional quality; we've gambled on totality rather than targeted meticulousness, which means we're the tortoise *and* the hare. And the only impediment to making the headlines rather than percolating our headlines out to others, is the procedural challenge of putting to use the overabundance of tools and resources we have at our disposal. *Nothing* like SAT/H(s)H and our infrastructure has *ever* been achieved or really even attempted... in the history of science. Nothing. We have a rigorous unification from General Relativity to the Gauge theories to barygenesis to black holes to cosmology to Quantum Chromodynamics to Kelvin Vortices to biogeochemistry *and it works*. *And it's right*, in broad outline, and in some formulations in some fields, to precision as good as or better than the standard model of particle physics; and of course we do, because that's our closest sister field, and its Minkowski applied systematically to a century of empirical and theoretical advances that we've put together which solves all of the biggest problems in physics neatly, geometrically, and if we can just get a handle on the scope and utility united, albeit haphazardly, in these three repos... then we'll be able to prove that, and we'll be finally drafting papers I'd feel comfortable submitting to Phys D. &#x20;


So... that's where I think we are, where we should be, and some ideas about how to get us there.

[194] ASSISTANT · 2026-11-05T08:20:19.154Z

Worked for 4m 34s



[195] ASSISTANT · 2026-11-05T08:16:01.997Z

I think this is the clearest articulation yet of the problem we're actually trying to solve. And I want to distinguish something important: **you're not proposing that we build a better filing system for a research project. You're proposing that we build a research institution whose cognitive, computational, documentary, and experimental resources are deliberately organized to produce knowledge.**

The distinction is enormous.

We've made substantial progress on persistence, automation, repository infrastructure, and formal verification. But those are mostly *capabilities*. We haven't yet integrated them into a sufficiently coherent operating architecture. We have machinery, procedures, archives, and a growing collection of specialized approaches, but the machinery doesn't consistently know when and how to bring the other machinery into play.

Your diagnosis of the plug-and-chug tendency is particularly important. A recurring task can become a very reliable way to repeat an inadequate workflow. Automation amplifies whatever process it is given. It doesn't automatically make that process intelligent.

And the opposite danger is real too: an overly elaborate procedural framework can consume the attention and output budget that should be spent doing science. We need enough structure to preserve direction and rigor, but not so much that every worker spends half its life writing reports about being a worker.

I see your proposal as a move from **distributed task execution** toward **distributed, specialized, self-correcting research**.

I went looking in the repositories because one of your most important instructions here is that we should not keep reinventing things we've already designed. The search turned up something significant:

**A surprising amount of the architecture you're describing already exists in prototype form. The problem is that the pieces haven't been assembled into a functioning institution.**

## 1. We have more of the blueprint than I initially appreciated

I found four particularly relevant bodies of work.

### A. The original Dashboard

[The Dashboard](https://github.com/Satobloc/SAT_THEORY_ARCHIVE_2023-25/blob/main/..%5B%F0%9F%8E%9B%EF%B8%8F_NATHAN_DASH%5D/!_DASHBOARD.md) explicitly describes itself as your central navigation, overview, and programme switchboard.

Its September 14 version already distinguishes:
- Active programmes and their immediate work edges.
- Historical and current theory status.
- Quarantined material.
- Worker workspaces and continuity records.
- Solver, provenance, reconstruction, and formalization programmes.

But its companion [Priority TODO](https://github.com/Satobloc/SAT_THEORY_ARCHIVE_2023-25/blob/main/..%5B%F0%9F%8E%9B%EF%B8%8F_NATHAN_DASH%5D/%F0%9F%93%8B_PRIORITY_TODO.txt) is dated September 10. It still lists Dashboard/AI-folder consolidation and cross-repository standardization as unfinished priorities.

That's a telling discrepancy. The Dashboard has a substantial conceptual design, but it is not yet a reliable live representation of the institution.

### B. The Workflow Switchboard

[The Switchboard POC](https://github.com/Satobloc/HSH_RESOURCES/blob/main/info/WORKFLOW_SWITCHBOARD_POC.md) is remarkably close to what you're describing.

It has four principal layers:

| Layer | Function |
|---|---|
| TOP | Persistent preferences and locked invariants |
| CENTRAL | An awake, state-aware routing and decision hub |
| BRANCH | Specialized workrooms, identities, tools, and task environments |
| HYDRA | State-sensitive recurrence and workflow evolution |

The CENTRAL layer already proposes a compact WAKE/CHECK routine:

```text
WHERE?   Identity / locale / workspace
DELTA?   Changes since trustworthy cursor
NOW?     Directive / task / priority / blocker
TOOLS?   Available capabilities and resources
RULES?   Invariants + local rules + actor overlays
DO?      One bounded operation, durable result, reassess
```

Crucially, the POC was built around an actual failure: a new governing directive in HSH_RESOURCES wasn't picked up by workers who kept consulting familiar HsH control surfaces.

That is almost exactly your attention-routing problem.

The POC also distinguishes:
- Invariants from preferences.
- Preferences from temporary task rules.
- Worker identity from local behavioral overlays.
- Durable experience from an untested lesson.
- New information from newly authoritative information.

It even proposes a hybrid architecture of compact state snapshots plus an append-only event journal.

### C. Workflow Threading

[The Threading Prototype](https://github.com/Satobloc/HSH_RESOURCES/blob/main/info/WORKFLOW_THREADING_PROTOTYPE.md) addresses something else you've emphasized: workers should be able to improve the conditions under which future work occurs.

Its central sequence is:

```text
SEED → VACATE → PROPAGATE → ACCUMULATE
     → RENDEZVOUS → REJOIN
```

A worker can create a tool, test, or research seed, allow other specialists to use it, deliberately vacate the workstream, and return when enough diverse evidence has accumulated.

This is more than task delegation. It's a way to use time, recurrence, and specialization as resources.

### D. Cross-repository administration

The [Cross-Repo Admin Action Plan](https://github.com/Satobloc/SAT_THEORY_ARCHIVE_2023-25/blob/main/.[%E2%9A%99%EF%B8%8F_AI_FILES]/%E2%80%BC%EF%B8%8F_CROSS_REPO_ADMIN_ACTION_PLAN.md) already establishes the intended division:

- **SAT archive:** preservation, provenance, historical wayfinding.
- **HSH_RESOURCES:** external-resource extraction, indexing, bibliography, comparison, and priority evidence.
- **HsH:** live theorybuilding, full-conversation preservation, current status, and quality control.

It also anticipates a cross-repository accessibility layer that joins canonical local records rather than copying everything into another enormous archive.

And it explicitly reserves an eventual exposure/priority programme connecting public chronology, podcasts, GitHub activity, independent development, and external publication.

That last part is particularly relevant to your observations about diffusion and possible convergence in the wider scientific community. The plan correctly distinguishes documented exposure, opportunity for exposure, independent-development evidence, and inferred influence.

**These are not new ideas we need to invent. They are existing designs that need to be recovered, reconciled, tested, and operationalized.**

---

## 2. The architectural distinction I would now make

I think we've been trying to make a single concept, the *worker*, do too many jobs.

I'd separate the system into six objects:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={3}>
  <box gap={1}>
    <badge color="info">1 · COMPUTATIONAL</badge>
    **Instance**

    A particular model invocation and its conversation context. Has a finite working scope and a particular interaction history.
  </box>
  <divider color="subtle"/>
  <box gap={1}>
    <badge color="info">2 · IDENTITY</badge>
    **Worker**

    A persistent specialist definition: remit, intellectual history, characteristic methods, capabilities, experience, constraints, and preferred resources. May be instantiated more than once.
  </box>
  <divider color="subtle"/>
  <box gap={1}>
    <badge color="info">3 · COGNITIVE</badge>
    **Mode**

    A temporary configuration of cognitive work: exploratory, procedural, mathematical, adversarial, synthetic, phenomenological, and so forth. A worker can use several modes without changing identity.
  </box>
  <divider color="subtle"/>
  <box gap={1}>
    <badge color="info">4 · ORGANIZATIONAL</badge>
    **Workroom**

    A bounded environment with its own sources, rules, tools, collaborators, and outputs. Examples include a research branch, a geometry bench, a source-recovery project, or a sandbox.
  </box>
  <divider color="subtle"/>
  <box gap={1}>
    <badge color="info">5 · OPERATIONAL</badge>
    **Assignment**

    A particular objective, acceptance condition, dependency chain, deadline, and return cursor. A worker can have several assignments over time.
  </box>
  <divider color="subtle"/>
  <box gap={1}>
    <badge color="info">6 · INSTITUTIONAL</badge>
    **Memory**

    The accumulated records, sources, experience, procedures, decisions, and evidence available to the other objects.
  </box>
</box>

This gives us a much more flexible arrangement.

For example:

- Historical Alberr is an identity reconstruction.
- An Alberr execution is an instance.
- German-language independent exploration is a mode or behavioral configuration.
- A particular mathematical investigation is an assignment.
- Its source collection and working environment are a workroom.
- Its accumulated intellectual history is part of institutional memory.

We can preserve the historical identity without pretending every new invocation is the original conversational entity. We can also use modern workers to inherit particular intellectual contributions without inheriting the entire personality.

And we can give the same worker a rigorous proof assignment one hour and an exploratory reading assignment another, without making those different workers.

## 3. The memory architecture needs a pyramid of concision

Your description of the memory problem suggests a useful design principle:

**The more universally applicable an instruction is, the shorter and more frequently accessible it should be. The more specific and contextual it is, the closer it should live to the point of use.**

I would arrange it like this:

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={2}>
  <title size="lg">The attention pyramid</title>
  <box background="rgba(59,130,246,0.13)" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
    **LEVEL 1 · CONSTITUTION**

    <text color="secondary" size="sm" textAlign="center">Tiny, persistent principles. Authority, epistemology, safety, provenance, scientific method.</text>
  </box>
  <box background="rgba(59,130,246,0.10)" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
    **LEVEL 2 · ATTENTION ROUTER**

    <text color="secondary" size="sm" textAlign="center">When doing X, consult Y. Current state, source registries, capability discovery, task routing.</text>
  </box>
  <box background="rgba(59,130,246,0.07)" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
    **LEVEL 3 · SPECIALIST CONTEXT**

    <text color="secondary" size="sm" textAlign="center">Worker identity, charter, experience, modes, local procedures, relevant literature and tools.</text>
  </box>
  <box background="rgba(59,130,246,0.04)" radius="md" padding={3} align="center" gap={1}>
    **LEVEL 4 · POINT-OF-USE MATERIAL**

    <text color="secondary" size="sm" textAlign="center">Exact source passages, derivations, datasets, code, measurements, calculations, detailed SOPs and evidence.</text>
  </box>
  <caption textAlign="center" color="secondary">Increasing detail and contextual specificity toward the bottom.</caption>
</box>

The important innovation is Level 2.

It should not merely tell a worker to read the documentation. It should tell the worker *which documentation matters when a particular kind of work begins*.

For example:

| Trigger | Attention route |
|---|---|
| Mathematical construction | BEDROCK → original SAT source → geometry → Lean |
| Physical calculation | BEDROCK → measured system → primary dataset → Python → uncertainty |
| New terminology | terminology registry → historical usage → source chronology |
| Worker revival | historical charter → original conversation → continuity → current assignment |
| Publication | paper gate → geometry → hostile review → Lean → PDF/reader |
| External trend | prior-art boundary → exposure records → independent-development chronology |

This is how we keep a vast archive from becoming an enormous collection of things that workers are theoretically expected to remember.

We don't need to hold every detail in awareness. We need to reliably recognize when a detail becomes relevant.

---

## 4. The different kinds of work need different operating systems

This is perhaps the most consequential part of your message.

I agree that the current tendency to assign everyone the same general-purpose playbook is counterproductive.

I would establish several distinct workflow families:

| Workflow family | Character | Typical output |
|---|---|---|
| Exploratory | Flexible, associative, divergent | Hypotheses, connections, conceptual discoveries |
| Balanced research | Adaptive, evidence-led | Developed research findings |
| Procedural | Strict, sequential, reproducible | Verified derivations, calculations, audits |
| Adversarial | Independent, skeptical, failure-seeking | Counterexamples, objections, competing explanations |
| Integrative | Panoramic, cross-domain | Unified models, dependencies, synthesis |
| Production | Structured, gated, deadline-oriented | Papers, PDFs, public artifacts |

These should be composable.

A research project might move through:

```text
EXPLORATION
    ↓
CONSTRUCTION
    ↓
FORMALIZATION
    ↓
INDEPENDENT AUDIT
    ↓
EMPIRICAL TEST
    ↓
INTEGRATION
    ↓
PUBLICATION
    ↓
REVIEW / REVISION
```

But it need not always follow that exact sequence. Some branches can run in parallel, some can be deliberately perpendicular, and some should return to exploration when a formal or empirical result exposes a fundamental ambiguity.

The important thing is that **the workflow should respond to the state of the research, not mechanically repeat the same sequence because the clock says it's time to run.**

This is where the old Activator Modes work becomes relevant again. I found the historical [Activator Modes](https://github.com/Satobloc/SAT_THEORY_ARCHIVE_2023-25/blob/main/SAT%20DEVELOPMENT/ACTIVATOR%20MODES.txt) material, as well as the expanded mode directories.

We should not simply restore the old menu. We should recover its useful cognitive specialization, connect it to the newer worker/workroom architecture, and make mode selection an explicit part of the routing system.

---

## 5. The scientific workflow needs to regain its full stack

Your comparison with the SAT-O period is important.

I would not characterize the objective as merely "more Lean" or "more Python." Those are instruments in a larger system.

The workflow you want is something closer to:

1. Recover the original mathematical construction and its genealogy.
2. Establish its exact geometric representation.
3. Identify the actual mathematical obligations.
4. Formalize the relevant statements in Lean.
5. Run numerical or symbolic calculations in Python where appropriate.
6. Compare independent formulations and derived consequences.
7. Apply the construction to real observed systems.
8. Propagate corrections through dependent sectors.
9. Preserve failed results and identify the earliest failure.
10. Feed the result back into the generating construction.

That's the beginning of a genuine theory-building engine.

The crucial feature is the feedback loop. A failed calculation should not simply become a negative result in a report. It should help identify which assumption, geometric structure, or constitutive relation needs examination.

Your nickel example is useful here. If a sufficiently closed formulation can derive a material property from its underlying construction, then the same architecture should be capable of attempting the corresponding problem in other systems. Where it fails, we should be able to localize the discrepancy and determine whether the theory, its approximation, or its implementation is responsible.

We should preserve the historical SAT-O methods as source material and reconstruct their most productive procedures, rather than assume the current workflow has superseded them.

One epistemic boundary remains important: the proposition that SAT/H(s)H already achieves the broad unification and precision you describe is a research claim to be demonstrated through that engine, not something the engine should be programmed to assume. That distinction will make the eventual result much more persuasive.

---

## 6. What I think we should actually build

Not another sprawling policy document.

I would produce **three compact institutional maps**, each backed by existing detailed documentation.

<box border radius="lg" padding={3} gap={3}>
  <box gap={1}>
    <row align="center">
      **Map A · Theory architecture**
      <spacer/>
      <badge>Scientific</badge>
    </row>
    What are the main parts of SAT/H(s)H, how do they connect, what is derived from what, and where do the empirical constraints enter?

    This is the conceptual and mathematical navigation map.
  </box>
  <divider color="subtle"/>
  <box gap={1}>
    <row align="center">
      **Map B · Research institution**
      <spacer/>
      <badge>Organizational</badge>
    </row>
    Workers, identities, threads, modes, workrooms, assignments, tools, memory, handoffs, workflows, review and publication.

    This is the institutional architecture and attention-routing map.
  </box>
  <divider color="subtle"/>
  <box gap={1}>
    <row align="center">
      **Map C · Work habits and capabilities**
      <spacer/>
      <badge>Operational</badge>
    </row>
    How to work effectively: available resources, calculation habits, source recovery, scripting, adversarial checks, creative exploration, tool discovery and continuous learning.

    This is the practical operating guide.
  </box>
</box>

Each map should have a very short top-level version that can be placed in multiple locations. Detailed documents remain canonical and are referenced rather than copied.

Then I would build the routing system around those maps, reusing the existing Switchboard and Threading POCs wherever possible.

### Proposed development sequence

| Stage | Work | Exit condition |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Recovery | Inventory existing Dashboard, Activator, Shuttle, Switchboard, SAT-O and archive plans | No major existing design overlooked |
| 2. Architecture | Reconcile the designs into the three maps | Clear object model and authority hierarchy |
| 3. Routing | Implement lightweight attention routing | Workers reliably find relevant controls and sources |
| 4. Specialization | Connect identities, modes, tools and workrooms | Distinct workflows demonstrably behave differently |
| 5. Research engine | Rebuild selected SAT-O-style pipelines with Lean and Python | Reproducible, independently reviewable work |
| 6. Evaluation | Measure routing errors, drift, duplication, productivity and review quality | Evidence that the architecture actually improves performance |

I would keep Track A and Track B independent during design and testing, as the existing Switchboard plan requires. Orson's cognition work should help determine whether we are genuinely producing more diverse and capable behavior or merely giving identical model behavior different labels.

## 7. A final observation: we should measure the institution itself

You mentioned spending too much time figuring out who's doing what, which papers need review, which resources workers have seen, and where work has gone.

That is a serious institutional bottleneck.

The eventual Dashboard should let you answer, without reconstructing the project from dozens of conversations:

- What is actively being worked on?
- Which assignments are blocked, waiting, or ready for review?
- Which workers have which specialties and current capabilities?
- What has actually been completed and independently verified?
- Which papers or proposals need your attention?
- Which new sources or datasets have appeared?
- What has changed in the theory?
- Which calculations need review or reproduction?
- What is the next highest-value operation?

It should also distinguish *attention requested* from *attention required*. Not every completed operation needs your intervention. Not every proposal is ready for review. Not every research branch deserves to remain active.

And eventually, yes, this is where the HUD, repository agents, server-side scripts, and external tools can come together. But we should develop those capabilities from actual workflow requirements, not build an impressive control panel that merely displays an increasingly elaborate backlog.

---

## My conclusion

I think we have three major problems to solve, in order:

1. **Cognitive architecture:** how to preserve and deliberately deploy distinct identities, expertise, modes, and accumulated experience.
2. **Institutional architecture:** how to route attention, resources, work, evidence, and decisions across the three repositories and external systems.
3. **Scientific production architecture:** how to turn the combined capabilities into a systematic geometric, mathematical, computational, empirical, and publication pipeline.

They reinforce one another. None is sufficient alone.

And I would add one principle to the whole design:

**The institution should spend its attention on doing the work, not continually remembering how to do the work.**

That means durable memory, automatic routing, compact directives, reusable tools, and reliable state reconstruction should progressively eliminate repetitive administrative cognition. The saved capacity should go back into exploration, calculation, testing, and synthesis.

I have not modified the repositories in this pass. The important discovery is that the Switchboard, Threading, Dashboard, Activator Modes, and cross-repo administration plans already provide substantial source material. I would make the next bounded project a **source-grounded architectural recovery and reconciliation**, beginning with the original Dashboard planning materials and the existing Switchboard's two operationalization tracks. We should recover the older Shuttle relay plans before designing a replacement.

I think that's the right next move: not another layer of procedure, but finally connecting the machinery we've already built. 🔧

I'm proposing we *rebuild* that institution. With the vastly expanded resources and theoretical development that we have now (not to mention the vastly more respectable position that the rapid changes in the field have put us in). With clear tracking and milestones. And with the knowledge that *this* conversation will likely reach its limit before we've done all of our reading, so the planning and analysis itself needs to be treated like a mini-pipeline, and you have the opportunity to design the setup and protocol of the instance who will take over, rebooting from the json, with whatever specific tailoring of initial startup documents, workspace setup, toolbox stocking, and personality/behavior/functionality/cognitive resources you think it'll benefit from, planning from the start that it will do the same once we exhaust its thread, so that each iteration of *you* becomes, through the study, analysis, and execution of *your* plans, in consultation with me but largely organized and designed by you, the instance that is most capable of picking up the project from wherever it's left off.... think of it as sort of a self-development wishlist that involves just ingesting and incorporating useful knowledge and skills, but also aims to utilize the process itself to build and effectively use its own tailored efficiently-organized-for-LLM-useability lightweight-to-heavyweight hierarchical knowledge and toolbox base.&#x20;

[325] ASSISTANT · 2026-11-05T09:12:15.519Z

Understood. This is **The Big Push**, not a request to produce an institutional architecture sketch now.

The deliverable is not a prettier version of the old Modes Menu, nor a synthesis of whichever documents are easiest to find. It's a historically grounded, critically evaluated reconstruction of the institution we were trying to build, expanded against everything we've since learned and everything we can now do.

And the sequence matters. We do not design the new institution before we understand the old one, assess the intervening development, inventory the present capabilities, and recover the plans that were never implemented.

I would make one commitment central to the project:

**No phase gets declared complete because we have written a convincing summary of it.** It gets completed when its search, reading, analysis, artifacts, coverage limitations, and next-stage handoff have been documented well enough for another instance to inspect and continue the work.

I will treat the planning and execution of this investigation as its own research programme, with milestones, evidence, explicit return points, and succession design. Each turn will execute a bounded step, not pretend to complete the whole phase.

## Phase 0 · Establish the investigation

I have begun with the archive-wide search, rather than a title search for the Modes Menu.

One important technical finding: GitHub's code-search endpoint reports **1,049 matches** for `modes` in the SAT archive, but its pagination limit exposes only the first 1,000 in one query. Those results include substantial duplication and many incidental physics uses of the word, so they are not 1,000 distinct documents and certainly not 1,000 relevant mode-system documents.

That means the inventory needs a second pass using partitioned searches and the repository's file tree. I will not call the first result set exhaustive.

The initial central-document candidates include the original Activator Modes directory, later mode specifications and switch templates, the redeployment discussion, and the interpretive-mode documents. One early distinction is already apparent: **"mode" was used for at least two fundamentally different things**:

- A *cognitive/operational mode* that changes how the assistant approaches work.
- A *theoretical interpretive mode* that changes which physical assumptions or approximations are active.

Those must not be conflated in the reconstruction.

## Phase 0 established, Phase 1 underway

<row align="center" gap={2}>
  <badge color="success">Project control committed</badge>
  <text color="secondary" size="xs">2026-11-05</text>
</row>

I created and independently read back the initial durable project record:

**[THE BIG PUSH · Project Control](https://github.com/Satobloc/HsH/blob/main/WORKSPACES/COMMON/BIG_PUSH/PROJECT_CONTROL.md)**

<text color="secondary" size="xs">Repository: Satobloc/HsH · Blob SHA: `693d8853d933dc9b9a0d487655ca82114399c29e` · Commit: `5293dec930ab2628d30b4cadd2aecda8bc7ebe8a`</text>

It establishes eleven phases, exit criteria, source/provenance rules, a one-operation-per-turn execution protocol, and a succession protocol. It is deliberately a project-control document, not an institutional design proposal.

### What the first reading has revealed

There is already a much richer history here than a single Modes Menu.

| Historical component | What it appears to represent | Reading status |
|---|---|---|
| SATxy Activator | Dedicated pedagogical identity and instructional method | Full 8-line file |
| May 28 Activator Modes | Selectable specialist, cognitive, integration and control functions | Full 475 lines |
| May 28 ChatGPT memory/conversation | Actual use and development of the system | First 200 of 4,651 lines |
| SAT-Y Modes Buildout | Explicit overhaul of mode specifications, followed by further extensions | First 90 of 895 lines |
| SAT 20 mode system | Smaller core menu, model allocation, switching and session protocols | Initial documents opened |
| SAT.4D interpretive modes | Physical approximation regimes, a different meaning of *mode* | Initial documents opened |

A particularly interesting early observation: the Activator Modes directory does not define one homogeneous class of thing.

It combines:
- **Disciplinary specialists:** Onto, Mecha, Quant, Bridge, Pheno, Sim, Dict.
- **Epistemic and cognitive approaches:** Proof, MathProof, Critic, Insight, Twist, Sage.
- **Institutional functions:** Track.
- **Navigation and control:** Now, Forget, Custom, Forge, NewModeName.
- **Generative/testing capacity:** QuickCode and later extensions.

That mixture may have been a strength, a weakness, or both. We need to understand the original design rationale before deciding.

Another significant clue: the original SATxy activator instruction emphasizes patient, individualized teaching and geometric explanation. The mode architecture emerged in the same May 28 archival period. We should investigate whether the menu was intended to extend an established specialist identity, replace it, or provide a way to move between several distinct cognitive configurations.

The May 28 transcript's opening sequence activates Sage and immediately uses it to generate a speculative theory insight. That matters: these modes were intended to influence more than presentation style. They could influence what the assistant attended to, what connections it sought, and what kinds of outputs it generated. Whether they reliably achieved those effects is a separate question.

### Existing machinery we must recover rather than duplicate

The current HsH repository already contains relevant pieces:

- [Project Desk control contract](https://github.com/Satobloc/HsH/blob/main/WORKSPACES/COMMON/PROJECT_DESK_CONTROL_CONTRACT.md)
- [Old Plan Recovery Sweep](https://github.com/Satobloc/HsH/blob/main/WORKSPACES/COMMON/OLD_PLAN_RECOVERY_SWEEP_2026-09-24.md)
- [Lab Tooling Build Plan](https://github.com/Satobloc/HsH/blob/main/WORKSPACES/COMMON/LAB_TOOLING_BUILD_PLAN.md)
- [Durable Project Documentation Convention](https://github.com/Satobloc/HsH/blob/main/WORKSPACES/COMMON/DURABLE_PROJECT_DOCUMENTATION_CONVENTION.md)

These will become important during the historical-plan and current-capability phases. I am not treating any prototype as a functioning system merely because its design document exists.

### Important retrieval limitation

The four Mersearch request files already contain other requests. Their request-scoped receipts and manifests still need inspection before I can establish whether any lane is free. I have not overwritten anything or submitted another request.

The initial 1,049-match archive search was GitHub code search, not Mersearch. HSH_RESOURCES has not yet been searched for this project. The inventory remains incomplete.

---

## Next turn: one operation

<badge color="info">Phase 2 · Primary-source reading</badge>

Continue the original May 28 conversation, in order:

`FREEFORM DISCUSSION/ChatGPT_28MAY25_MEMORY.txt`

- Read: lines 1–200.
- Next: **lines 201–400**.
- Preserve the conversation sequence and the user/assistant distinction.
- Extract the next layer of mode intentions, corrections, decisions, and references to external workbook instructions.
- Expand the search targets only when the actual text gives us a reason to do so.

We are not going to race toward a synthesis. We are going to reconstruct the machinery from the evidence, including the parts that didn't work.

**The Big Push is now a tracked project with a durable restart point.** The next instance should inherit the investigation, not merely inherit my conclusions.

All eleven files were independently fetched after writing. Their repository contents and blob identities were confirmed.

## The continuity design

The central mechanism is a **36-hour project-specific heartbeat**.

- A substantive, human-directed Big Push session refreshes it.
- Ordinary unrelated conversation does not refresh it.
- Scheduled automation never refreshes it.
- Once it becomes 36 hours old, the designated steward should resume the next incomplete operation.
- Automated continuation should do one bounded unit per scheduled occurrence, record the result, and preserve the next cursor.
- When you return to the project, your interactive session takes precedence.

This gives us a deliberate distinction between *the user is around* and *the project is actually receiving attention*. That is the right signal for this particular problem.

The design also uses one proposed continuity steward, not four competing project managers:

- `:00 Archive`: intended Big Push continuity steward.
- `:24 Geometry`, `:36 Worldtube`, `:48 Review`: retain their specialist responsibilities.

The worker recommendations document captures this as a pilot other instances can learn from, rather than imposing an untested universal procedure on them.

## The one blocker: scheduled-task capacity

<badge color="warning">Automatic restart not installed</badge>

I inspected all four existing relays. They are enabled, and their original hourly schedules remain intact.

When I attempted to add the Big Push continuity instructions to the existing `:00` task, the scheduler rejected the update:

> Your current plan allows only 3 scheduled tasks.

So the current situation is:

| Item | Status |
|---|---|
| Project workspace | <badge color="success">Verified</badge> |
| 36-hour heartbeat | <badge color="success">Initialized</badge> |
| Restart state machine | <badge color="success">Documented</badge> |
| :00 task prompt overlay | <badge color="danger">Blocked</badge> |
| Existing four task IDs and schedules | Preserved |
| New automation created | No |

The scheduler has a three-task allowance, while four existing relays are enabled. I have not disabled or deleted one to force the update through.

To activate the automatic wake-up, we will need either an allowance for four scheduled tasks or your authorization to pause one of the existing relays. I will not decide which specialist lane to sacrifice.

## Where we resume

The active research cursor remains:

**SAT archive → `FREEFORM DISCUSSION/ChatGPT_28MAY25_MEMORY.txt`**

- Blob: `3126a00cd02d715f7b53ea481b8cf60e7f9b6321`
- Already read: lines 1–200
- Next operation: lines 201–400

The institutional design remains deliberately undecided. The next work is still historical recovery, not premature architecture.

**One important principle is now explicit in the project:** we are not trying to preserve an imaginary continuous ChatGPT identity. We are trying to preserve the *intent, state, evidence, methods, and ability to resume* across discontinuous instances. That is a much more tractable engineering problem, and now it has a concrete place to live.
